I’ll be trying to pay attention or just do what I’m supposed to do, but my whole body feels like it has to move.\n\nI’ve tried to just tough it out for months, telling myself I should be able to handle it, but I still end up pacing, shifting around in my seat, standing up, or finding some excuse to move even when it’s probably not the right time. It’s gotten to the point where it feels like I need to talk to someone about it instead of just pretending it’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Did this restless feeling start after you began a medication or changed the dose of one?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, I’m trying to think through it carefully, because I know sometimes timing matters with that stuff, but I can’t point to a specific medication change where it suddenly started or got way worse right after.\n\nI mean, I don’t really have a clear “oh, I started this pill and then the restless feeling began” kind of thing. It’s more like… this has just been there, and I’ve been dealing with it, and it’s gotten hard to ignore. But no, not that I’ve noticed with starting something new or changing a dose.\n\nDoctor: Since you were a child, have you often had trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, that part goes way back. My parents still bring up stuff from elementary school, like parent-teacher conferences where the teacher would say I was constantly out of my seat, or I’d call out before I was supposed to, or I’d rush ahead before they even finished giving the directions.\n\nAnd that was not, like, a recent thing from radiology training or college stress or something. It was happening way before middle school. I kind of grew up hearing, “You need to stay seated,” or “Wait until they finish explaining,” so… yeah, trouble sitting still has been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high or irritable?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. Like, I’ve definitely had times where I’m irritated or kind of wound up, but I don’t know if I’d call it an “unusually high” mood or anything like that.\n\nWhen you say “period,” do you mean like days where I’m noticeably different from normal, or just moments where I’m in a really good mood or snap at people? Because I can get frustrated, especially when I’m feeling stuck or like I can’t move around, but I don’t know that I’ve had some separate stretch where my mood was, like, abnormally elevated. I’m not saying impossible, I just… nothing obvious comes to mind the way you’re describing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on tasks or conversations?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just being tired or bored, honestly. I mean, I do notice my mind can kind of drift if something is really repetitive or if someone is talking for a long time, but I don’t know if it’s more than what other people deal with.\n\nIn conversations, sometimes I’ll realize I’m thinking ahead about what I’m going to say, or I’m looking at something else in the room, and then I’m like, wait, I should probably be listening better. But I wouldn’t know how to rate whether that’s “often” or clinically a problem, if that makes sense.\n\nFor tasks, it depends a lot on what the task is. If it’s something interesting or hands-on, it’s easier. If it’s just sitting there reading or going through something slow, then yeah, my focus is not great. But I don’t know if I’d say I have a clear answer beyond that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not usually, no. I mean, I can be stressed sometimes, like anyone, but the “I can’t sit still” feeling doesn’t really feel like worry to me. It’s more physical, like I’m crawling out of my skin or there’s this pressure in me to get up and move.

That’s honestly a big reason I came in. In the radiology suite or in class, when I’m supposed to stay in one spot and just sit there or stand still and wait, I start shifting around, bouncing my leg, pacing if I can, or I’ll find some excuse to go do something even if it’s not really necessary. I’ve tried for months to just tough it out and tell myself to stay put, but it still happens. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “Oh no, something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like my body just won’t let me be still.

Yeah, but I don’t know if it’s like a “period” in the way you’re asking, like a sudden episode where I’m a totally different person. It’s more like… most days I just keep moving from one thing to the next.

Like I’ll go from class to clinicals, then to the gym, then I’ll run errands, and if there’s any empty space in the day, I almost automatically fill it with something. Even if I technically could go home and relax, I’ll think, okay, I should stop at the store, or clean my car, or go work out, or do something, because just sitting around feels almost impossible. It’s not really that I’m in some amazing mood or feeling unstoppable. It’s more like if I stop, I get really uncomfortable, so I keep adding stuff to my day.

So yes, my energy is high a lot, but it feels kind of constant rather than like one specific unusual stretch.

acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: Most days I go from class to clinicals to the gym to errands without really stopping, and if there’s a gap in the schedule I immediately fill it with something because sitting around feels almost impossible.\n- key: S016\n  name: Blurting_Answers\n  description: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n  manifestation: When a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question, I’ll jump in with the answer before they finish, and then I realize I missed the part that actually mattered.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting my turn during lab practice or for a machine to open up makes me so edgy that I keep stepping forward, checking the time, and asking if I can go next, which has gotten me called out more than once.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: were finished. That was happening way before middle school, not just since radiology training.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My clinical evaluations have taken a hit, and I was put on a formal remediation plan because supervisors say my behavior disrupts patient flow and makes teamwork harder. I’m worried it could cost me my place in the program if I don’t get it under control.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been causing serious problems since I switched into radiologic technology training about 1 year and 5 months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / radiologic_technologist_or_technician / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S012\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you feel like you can’t sit still, is it usually because you’re feeling anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean, I can be stressed sometimes, like anyone, but the “I can’t sit still” feeling doesn’t really feel like worry to me. It’s more physical, like I’m crawling out of my skin or there’s this pressure in me to get up and move.\n\nThat’s honestly a big reason I came in. In the radiology suite or in class, when I’m supposed to stay in one spot and just sit there or stand still and wait, I start shifting around, bouncing my leg, pacing if I can, or I’ll find some excuse to go do something even if it’s not really necessary. I’ve tried for months to just tough it out and tell myself to stay put, but it still happens. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “Oh no, something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like my body just won’t let me be still.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your energy was much higher than usual and you were doing a lot more than normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, but I don’t know if it’s like a “period” in the way you’re asking, like a sudden episode where I’m a totally different person. It’s more like… most days I just keep moving from one thing to the next.\n\nLike I’ll go from class to clinicals, then to the gym, then I’ll run errands, and if there’s any empty space in the day, I almost automatically fill it with something. Even if I technically could go home and relax, I’ll think, okay, I should stop at the store, or clean my car, or go work out, or do something, because just sitting around feels almost impossible. It’s not really that I’m in some amazing mood or feeling unstoppable. It’s more like if I stop, I get really uncomfortable, so I keep adding stuff to my day.\n\nSo yes, my energy is high a lot, but it feels kind of constant rather than like one specific unusual stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a distinct period when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. Like, I’ve definitely had nights where I didn’t sleep enough because of school or clinical stuff or just staying up too late, and then the next day I kind of push through because I have to. But I wouldn’t say I felt fully energized in a weird way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nUsually if I get less sleep, I can feel it. I might still be moving around a lot, because that’s kind of how I am, but it doesn’t feel like I magically don’t need sleep. I’ll feel tired or foggy underneath it, even if I’m still restless. So no, I can’t really think of a distinct stretch where I only needed a few hours and felt great or unusually powered up.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often have trouble getting organized and finishing tasks?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it depends what you mean by organized. I don’t think of that as the main problem I’m here for, if that makes sense.

Like, I’ve definitely had nights where I didn’t sleep enough because of school or clinical stuff or just staying up too late, and then the next day I kind of push through because I have to. But I wouldn’t say I felt fully energized in a weird way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nUsually if I get less sleep, I can feel it. I might still be moving around a lot, because that’s kind of how I am, but it doesn’t feel like I magically don’t need sleep. I’ll feel tired or foggy underneath it, even if I’m still restless. So no, I can’t really think of a distinct stretch where I only needed a few hours and felt great or unusually powered up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble getting organized and finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it depends what you mean by organized. I don’t think of that as the main problem I’m here for, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I can get through things when there’s a clear structure or when I know exactly what has to happen, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m more disorganized than other people. And with finishing tasks… I mean, I finish things I have to finish, but I’d have to think about it more to know if it’s actually a pattern or just normal school/work stress.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say “often” without thinking through examples.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you spend a lot of time worrying about different things?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I mean… I worry about things, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say I spend a lot of time worrying about all different kinds of things. It’s not like I’m constantly sitting there thinking something terrible is going to happen, at least not that I’ve really noticed.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I just cannot sit still when I’m supposed to. Like in the radiology suite, or even in class, it feels like I’m crawling out of my skin if I have to stay in one place too long. I’ll be trying to pay attention or just do what I’m supposed to do, but my whole body feels like it has to move.\n\nI’ve tried to just tough it out for months, telling myself I should be able to handle it, but I still end up pacing, shifting around in my seat, standing up, or finding some excuse to move even when it’s probably not the right time. It’s gotten to the point where it feels like I need to talk to someone about it instead of just pretending it’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Did this restless feeling start after you began a medication or changed the dose of one?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, I’m trying to think through it carefully, because I know sometimes timing matters with that stuff, but I can’t point to a specific medication change where it suddenly started or got way worse right after.\n\nI mean, I don’t really have a clear “oh, I started this pill and then the restless feeling began” kind of thing. It’s more like… this has just been there, and I’ve been dealing with it, and it’s gotten hard to ignore. But no, not that I’ve noticed with starting something new or changing a dose.\n\nDoctor: Since you were a child, have you often had trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, that part goes way back. My parents still bring up stuff from elementary school, like parent-teacher conferences where the teacher would say I was constantly out of my seat, or I’d call out before I was supposed to, or I’d rush ahead before they even finished giving the directions.\n\nAnd that was not, like, a recent thing from radiology training or college stress or something. It was happening way before middle school. I kind of grew up hearing, “You need to stay seated,” or “Wait until they finish explaining,” so… yeah, trouble sitting still has been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high or irritable?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. Like, I’ve definitely had times where I’m irritated or kind of wound up, but I don’t know if I’d call it an “unusually high” mood or anything like that.\n\nWhen you say “period,” do you mean like days where I’m noticeably different from normal, or just moments where I’m in a really good mood or snap at people? Because I can get frustrated, especially when I’m feeling stuck or like I can’t move around, but I don’t know that I’ve had some separate stretch where my mood was, like, abnormally elevated. I’m not saying impossible, I just… nothing obvious comes to mind the way you’re describing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on tasks or conversations?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just being tired or bored, honestly. I mean, I do notice my mind can kind of drift if something is really repetitive or if someone is talking for a long time, but I don’t know if it’s more than what other people deal with.\n\nIn conversations, sometimes I’ll realize I’m thinking ahead about what I’m going to say, or I’m looking at something else in the room, and then I’m like, wait, I should probably be listening better. But I wouldn’t know how to rate whether that’s “often” or clinically a problem, if that makes sense.\n\nFor tasks, it depends a lot on what the task is. If it’s something interesting or hands-on, it’s easier. If it’s just sitting there reading or going through something slow, then yeah, my focus is not great. But I don’t know if I’d say I have a clear answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you can’t sit still, is it usually because you’re feeling anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean, I can be stressed sometimes, like anyone, but the “I can’t sit still” feeling doesn’t really feel like worry to me. It’s more physical, like I’m crawling out of my skin or there’s this pressure in me to get up and move.\n\nThat’s honestly a big reason I came in. In the radiology suite or in class, when I’m supposed to stay in one spot and just sit there or stand still and wait, I start shifting around, bouncing my leg, pacing if I can, or I’ll find some excuse to go do something even if it’s not really necessary. I’ve tried for months to just tough it out and tell myself to stay put, but it still happens. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “Oh no, something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like my body just won’t let me be still.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your energy was much higher than usual and you were doing a lot more than normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, but I don’t know if it’s like a “period” in the way you’re asking, like a sudden episode where I’m a totally different person. It’s more like… most days I just keep moving from one thing to the next.\n\nLike I’ll go from class to clinicals, then to the gym, then I’ll run errands, and if there’s any empty space in the day, I almost automatically fill it with something. Even if I technically could go home and relax, I’ll think, okay, I should stop at the store, or clean my car, or go work out, or do something, because just sitting around feels almost impossible. It’s not really that I’m in some amazing mood or feeling unstoppable. It’s more like if I stop, I get really uncomfortable, so I keep adding stuff to my day.\n\nSo yes, my energy is high a lot, but it feels kind of constant rather than like one specific unusual stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a distinct period when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. Like, I’ve definitely had nights where I didn’t sleep enough because of school or clinical stuff or just staying up too late, and then the next day I kind of push through because I have to. But I wouldn’t say I felt fully energized in a weird way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nUsually if I get less sleep, I can feel it. I might still be moving around a lot, because that’s kind of how I am, but it doesn’t feel like I magically don’t need sleep. I’ll feel tired or foggy underneath it, even if I’m still restless. So no, I can’t really think of a distinct stretch where I only needed a few hours and felt great or unusually powered up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble getting organized and finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it depends what you mean by organized. I don’t think of that as the main problem I’m here for, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I can get through things when there’s a clear structure or when I know exactly what has to happen, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m more disorganized than other people. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I know everybody puts off studying or paperwork sometimes, so I have a hard time knowing where the line is between “normal procrastination” and it being a bigger pattern.

With reading or studying, I guess it depends on what it is and how much pressure there is. If it’s something I know I absolutely have to get done, I usually find some way to do it, even if it’s not pretty or perfectly planned out. But I don’t know if I’d say I avoid it because it takes mental effort, exactly. Sometimes I just don’t start right away, and then later I’m like, “Okay, now I really have to do this.” But I haven’t really tracked whether that happens often enough to call it a real thing.

Paperwork is kind of the same. I don’t love it, but I don’t know many people who do. So… maybe sometimes I put it off, but I’m not sure I can confidently say I often avoid those kinds of tasks. I’d probably need to think through specific examples.

In the radiology suite or in class, when I’m supposed to stay in one spot and just sit there or stand still and wait, I start shifting around, bouncing my leg, pacing if I can, or I’ll find some excuse to go do something even if it’s not really necessary. I’ve tried for months to just tough it out and tell myself to stay put, but it still happens. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “Oh no, something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like my body just won’t let me be still.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your energy was much higher than usual and you were doing a lot more than normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, but I don’t know if it’s like a “period” in the way you’re asking, like a sudden episode where I’m a totally different person. It’s more like… most days I just keep moving from one thing to the next.\n\nLike I’ll go from class to clinicals, then to the gym, then I’ll run errands, and if there’s any empty space in the day, I almost automatically fill it with something. Even if I technically could go home and relax, I’ll think, okay, I should stop at the store, or clean my car, or go work out, or do something, because just sitting around feels almost impossible. It’s not really that I’m in some amazing mood or feeling unstoppable. It’s more like if I stop, I get really uncomfortable, so I keep adding stuff to my day.\n\nSo yes, my energy is high a lot, but it feels kind of constant rather than like one specific unusual stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a distinct period when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. Like, I’ve definitely had nights where I didn’t sleep enough because of school or clinical stuff or just staying up too late, and then the next day I kind of push through because I have to. But I wouldn’t say I felt fully energized in a weird way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nUsually if I get less sleep, I can feel it. I might still be moving around a lot, because that’s kind of how I am, but it doesn’t feel like I magically don’t need sleep. I’ll feel tired or foggy underneath it, even if I’m still restless. So no, I can’t really think of a distinct stretch where I only needed a few hours and felt great or unusually powered up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble getting organized and finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it depends what you mean by organized. I don’t think of that as the main problem I’m here for, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I can get through things when there’s a clear structure or when I know exactly what has to happen, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m more disorganized than other people. And with finishing tasks… I mean, I finish things I have to finish, but I’d have to think about it more to know if it’s actually a pattern or just normal school/work stress.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say “often” without thinking through examples.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about different things?\n\nPatient: I mean… I worry about things, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say I spend a lot of time worrying about all different kinds of things. It’s not like I’m constantly sitting there thinking something terrible is going to happen, at least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI’ll stress about normal stuff, like school or whether I’m keeping up with what I’m supposed to do, but it feels more like regular pressure than me being stuck in worry all day. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to it, because I don’t usually think of myself as a super anxious person. If anything, I’m usually just trying to get through whatever’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often put off or avoid tasks that require sustained mental effort, like reading, paperwork, or studying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I know everybody puts off studying or paperwork sometimes, so I have a hard time knowing where the line is between “normal procrastination” and it being a bigger pattern.\n\nWith reading or studying, I guess it depends on what it is and how much pressure there is. If it’s something I know I absolutely have to get done, I usually find some way to do it, even if it’s not pretty or perfectly planned out. But I don’t know if I’d say I avoid it because it takes mental effort, exactly. Sometimes I just don’t start right away, and then later I’m like, “Okay, now I really have to do this.” But I haven’t really tracked whether that happens often enough to call it a real thing.\n\nPaperwork is kind of the same. I don’t love it, but I don’t know many people who do. So… maybe sometimes I put it off, but I’m not sure I can confidently say I often avoid those kinds of tasks. I’d probably need to think through specific examples.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not totally sure how to separate that from just being tired or bored, honestly. I mean, I do notice my mind can kind of drift if something is really repetitive or if someone is talking for a long time, but I don’t know if it’s more than what other people deal with.\n\nIn conversations, sometimes I’ll realize I’m thinking ahead about what I’m going to say, or I’m looking at something else in the room, and then I’m like, wait, I should probably be listening better. But I wouldn’t know how to rate whether that’s “often” or clinically a problem, if that makes sense.\n\nFor tasks, it depends a lot on what the task is. If it’s something interesting or hands-on, it’s easier. If it’s just sitting there reading or going through something slow, then yeah, my focus is not great. But I don’t know if I’d say I have a clear answer beyond that.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

(Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The question is asking about a distinct period of decreased need for sleep with high energy, which is more related to possible manic or hypomanic episodes. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking about excessive worry or anxiety across different topics, which is not covered by the provided ADHD hyperactive/impulsive symptom profiles.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to it, because I don’t usually think of myself as a super anxious person. If anything, I’m usually just trying to get through whatever’s in front of me.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not sure, honestly. Like, I know everybody puts off studying or paperwork sometimes, so I have a hard time knowing where the line is between “normal procrastination” and it being a bigger pattern.\n\nWith reading or studying, I guess it depends on what it is and how much pressure there is. If it’s something I know I absolutely have to get done, I usually find some way to do it, even if it’s not pretty or perfectly planned out. But I don’t know if I’d say I avoid it because it takes mental effort, exactly. Sometimes I just don’t start right away, and then later I’m like, “Okay, now I really have to do this.” But I haven’t really tracked whether that happens often enough to call it a real thing.\n\nPaperwork is kind of the same. I don’t love it, but I don’t know many people who do. So… maybe sometimes I put it off, but I’m not sure I can confidently say I often avoid those kinds of tasks. I’d probably need to think through specific examples.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
